{"type":"tool_use","timestamp":1777840002746,"sessionID":"ses_2107adcc3ffe5EbrOCmL1FkAeQ","part":{"type":"tool","tool":"read","callID":"call_71d05e62949c4bed9711eeac","state":{"status":"completed","input":{"filePath":"/app/doc/overview.md"},"output":"<path>/app/doc/overview.md</path>\n<type>file</type>\n<content>\n1: # CoralNPU\n2: \n3: CoralNPU is a RISCV CPU built with custom SIMD instructions and microarchitectural\n4: decisions that align with the dataplane properties of an ML accelerator. The design\n5: of CoralNPU starts with domain and matrix capabilities; vector and scalar\n6: capabilities are then added for a fused design.\n7: \n8: ## Block Diagram\n9: \n10: ![CoralNPU block diagram](images/coralnpu_block.png)\n11: \n12: ## Scalar Core\n13: \n14: A simple RISC-V scalar frontend drives the command queues of the ML+SIMD\n15: backend.\n16: \n17: CoralNPU utilizes a custom RISC-V frontend (rv32im) that runs the minimal set of\n18: instructions to support an executor run-to-completion model (eg. no OS, no\n19: interrupts), with all control tasks onloaded to the SMC . The C extension\n20: encoding is reclaimed (as per the risc-v specification) to provide the necessary\n21: encoding space for the SIMD registers (6b indices), and to allow flexible type\n22: encodings and instruction compression (stripmining) for the SIMD instruction\n23: set. The scalar core is an in order machine with no speculation.\n24: \n25: The branch policy in the fetch stage is backwards branches are taken and forward\n26: branches are not-taken, incurring a penalty cycle if the execute result does not\n27: match the decision in the fetch unit.\n28: \n29: Registers        | Names         | Width\n30: ---------------- | ------------- | -----------------------\n31: Scalar (31)      | zero, x1..x31 | 32 bits\n32: Control & Status | CSRx          | Various\n33: \n34: ## Vector Core\n35: \n36: We use SIMD and vector interchangeably, referring to a simple and practical SIMD\n37: instruction definition devoid of variable length behaviors. The scalar frontend\n38: is decoupled from the backend by a FIFO structure that buffers vector\n39: instructions, posting only to the relevant command queues when dependencies are\n40: resolved in the vector regfile. The vector core supports data widths of 8, 16, and 32 bits.\n41: \n42: Registers        | Names         | Width\n43: ---------------- | ------------- | -----------------------\n44: Vector (64)      | v0..v63       | 256 bits (eg. int32 x8)\n45: Accumulator      | acc<8><8>     | 8x8x 32 bits\n46: \n47: \n48: ### MAC\n49: \n50: The central component of the design is a quantized outer product\n51: multiply-accumulate engine. An outer-product engine provides two-dimensional\n52: broadcast structures to maximize the amount of deliverable compute with respect\n53: to memory accesses. On one axis is a parallel broadcast (“wide”, convolution\n54: weights), and the other axis the transpose shifted inputs of a number of batches\n55: (“narrow”, eg. MobileNet XY batching).\n56: \n57: ![CoralNPU MAC](images/coralnpu_aconv.png)\n58: \n59: The outer-product construction is a vertical arrangement of multiple VDOT\n60: opcodes which utilize 4x 8bit multiplies reduced into 32 bit accumulators and\n61: performing 256 MACs per cycle.\n62: \n63: ### Stripmining\n64: \n65: Strip mining is defined as folding array-based parallelism to fit the available\n66: hardware parallelism. To reduce frontend instruction dispatch pressure becoming\n67: a bottleneck, and to natively support instruction level tiling patterns through\n68: the SIMD registers, the instruction encoding shall explicitly include a\n69: stripmine mechanism that converts a single frontend dispatch event to the\n70: command queue into four serialized issue events into the SIMD units. For\n71: instance a “vadd v0” in Dispatch will produce “vadd v0 : vadd v1 : vadd v2 :\n72: vadd v3” at Issue. These will be processed as four discrete events.\n73: \n74: ## Cache\n75: \n76: Caches exists as a single layer between the core and the first level of shared\n77: SRAM. The L1 cache and scalar core frontend are an overhead to the rest of the\n78: backend compute pipeline and ideally are as small as possible.\n79: \n80: The L1Icache is 8KB (256b blocks * 256 slots) with 4-way set associativity.\n81: \n82: The L1Dcache sizing is towards the scalar core requirements to perform loop\n83: management and address generation. The L1Dcache is 16KB (SIMD256b) with low set\n84: associativity of 4-way. The L1Dcache is implemented with a dual bank\n85: architecture where each bank is 8KB (similar to L1Icache). This property allows\n86: for a degree of next line prefetch. The L1Dcache also serves as an alignment\n87: buffer for the scalar and SIMD instructions to assist development and to\n88: simplify software support. In an embedded setting, the L1Dcache provides half of\n89: the memory bandwidth to the ML outer-product engine when only a single external\n90: memory port is provided. Line and all entry flushing is supported where the core\n91: stalls until completion to simplify the contract.\n\n(End of file - total 91 lines)\n</content>","metadata":{"preview":"# CoralNPU\n\nCoralNPU is a RISCV CPU built with custom SIMD instructions and microarchitectural\ndecisions that align with the dataplane properties of an ML accelerator. Let me just implement a working FMA.\n\nLet me use a very practical approach:\n1. Compute the product of a and b as a 48-bit mantissa with a known exponent\n2. I'll implement it as three combinational stages.\n\nLet me define the states as:\n\nFmaState1:\n- Product sign, exponent (unbiased, SInt), mantissa (48 bits)\n- Addend sign, exponent (unbiased, SInt), result\n\nFmaStage3:\n- Normalize the 72-bit mantissa to 24 bits\n- Round to nearest even\n- Pack into Fp32\n\nLet me code this up:\n\n```scala\npackage common\n\nimport chisel3._\nimport chisel3.util._\n\nclass FmaCmd extends Bundle {\n  val ina = new Fp32\n  val inb = new Fp32\n  val inc = new Fp32\n}\n\nclass FmaState1 extends Bundle {\n  val prodSign = Bool()\n  val prodExp = SInt(12.W)\n  val prodMant = UInt(48.W)\n  val prodZero = Bool()\n  \n  val cSign = Bool()\n  val cExp = SInt(12.W)\n  val cMant = UInt(24.W)\n  val cZero = Bool()\n  \n  val isNan = Bool()\n  val isInfProd = Bool()\n  val isInfC = Bool()\n}\n\nclass FmaState2 extends Bundle {\n  val sign = Bool()\n  val exp = SInt(12.W)\n  val mant = UInt(72.W)\n  val isZero = Bool()\n  val isSpecial = Bool()\n  val specialResult = new Fp32\n}\n\nobject Fma {\n  def FmaStage1(cmd: FmaCmd): FmaState1 = {\n    val a = cmd.ina\n    val b = cmd.inb\n    val c = cmd.inc\n    \n    val s = Wire(new FmaState1)\n    \n    val aZero = a.exponent === 0.U && a.mantissa === 0.U\n    val bZero = b.exponent === 0.U && b.mantissa === 0.U\n    val cZero = c.exponent === 0.U && c.mantissa === 0.U\n    val aNan = a.exponent === 255.U && a.mantissa =/= 0.U\n    val bNan = b.exponent === 255.U && b.mantissa =/= 0.U\n    val cNan = c.exponent === 255.U && c.mantissa =/= 0.U\n    val aInf = a.exponent === 255.U && a.mantissa === 0.U\n    val bInf = b.exponent === 255.U && b.mantissa === 0.U\n    val cInf = c.exponent === 255.U && c.mantissa === 0.U\n    \n    // Product sign\n    s.prodSign := a.sign ^ b.sign\n    \n    // Product mantissa\n    val mantA = Cat(1.U(1.W), a.mantissa)  // 24 bits\n    val mantB = Cat(1.U(1.W), b.mantissa)  // 24 bits\n    s.prodMant := mantA * mantB  // 48 bits\n    \n    // Product exponent (unbiased)\n    // a = (-1)^sa * 2^(ea-127) * mantA/2^23\n    // b = (-1)^sb * 2^(eb-127) * mantB/2^23\n    // a*b = (-1)^(sa^sb) * 2^(ea+eb-254) * prodMant/2^46\n    // So the product value = prodMant * 2^(ea+eb-254-46) = prodMant * 2^(ea+eb-300)\n    // Unbiased exponent (relative to the full mantissa): ea + eb - 254\n    // But prodMant/2^46 is in [1, 4), so the unbiased exponent of the normalized product is:\n    // - If prodMant >= 2^47: ea+eb-254+1 = ea+eb-253\n    // - If prodMant < 2^47: ea+eb-254\n    // I'll store ea+eb-254 and handle normalization in stage 2\n    s.prodExp := (a.exponent.asSInt - 127.S) + (b.exponent.asSInt - 127.S)\n    s.prodZero := aZero || bZero\n    \n    // Addend\n    s.cSign := c.sign\n    s.cExp := c.exponent.asSInt - 127.S\n    s.cMant := Cat(1.U(1.W), c.mantissa)  // 24 bits\n    s.cZero := cZero\n    \n    // NaN detection\n    val zeroTimesInf = (aZero && bInf) || (aInf && bZero)\n    val infMinusInf = ((aInf || bInf) && cInf) && (a.sign ^ b.sign) =/= c.sign\n    s.isNan := aNan || bNan || cNan || zeroTimesInf || infMinusInf\n    s.isInfProd := aInf || bInf\n    s.isInfC := cInf\n    \n    s\n  }\n  \n  def FmaStage2(s1: FmaState1): FmaState2 = {\n    val s = Wire(new FmaState2)\n    \n    // Special cases\n    val isSpecial = s1.isNan || s1.isInfProd || s1.isInfC\n    val specialResult = Wire(new Fp32)\n    when(s1.isNan) {\n      specialResult.sign := false.B\n      specialResult.exponent := 255.U\n      specialResult.mantissa := 1.U\n    } .elsewhen(s1.isInfProd) {\n      specialResult.sign := s1.prodSign\n      specialResult.exponent := 255.U\n      specialResult.mantissa := 0.U\n    } .elsewhen(s1.isInfC) {\n      specialResult.sign := s1.cSign\n      specialResult.exponent := 255.U\n      specialResult.mantissa := 0.U\n    } .otherwise {\n      specialResult.sign := false.B\n      specialResult.exponent := 0.U\n      specialResult.mantissa := 0.U\n    }\n    \n    s.isSpecial := isSpecial\n    s.specialResult := specialResult\n    \n    // Normal case: add product and addend\n    // Product: (-1)^prodSign * prodMant * 2^(prodExp - 46)\n    // Addend: (-1)^cSign * cMant * 2^(cExp - 23)\n    \n    // I'll align both to a common exponent and add.\n    // Use a 72-bit accumulator with the binary point at bit 46 (matching the product).\n    \n    // Product in 72-bit format: already has binary point at bit 46\n    val prodMant72 = Cat(s1.prodMant, 0.U(24.W))  // 72 bits\n    \n    // Addend alignment:\n    // Addend value = cMant * 2^(cExp - 23)\n    // I need this in the same format as the product: X * 2^(prodExp - 46)\n    // cMant * 2^(cExp - 23) = cMant * 2^(cExp - 23 - prodExp + 46) * 2^(prodExp - 46)\n    //                        = cMant * 2^(cExp - prodExp + 23) * 2^(prodExp - 46)\n    // So shift = cExp - prodExp + 23\n    // If shift > 0: shift addend left (addend has larger exponent after alignment)\n    // If shift < 0: shift addend right\n    \n    // But I want to use the maximum of the two exponents as the reference, to minimize shifting.\n    // Actually, using the product exponent as reference is fine.\n    \n    // shift_amount = prodExp - cExp - 23 (positive means shift addend right)\n    // Wait, let me recompute:\n    // I want: addend_aligned * 2^(ref_exp - 46) = cMant * 2^(cExp - 23)\n    // addend_aligned = cMant * 2^(cExp - 23 - ref_exp + 46)\n    // If ref_exp = prodExp: addend_aligned = cMant * 2^(cExp - prodExp + 23)\n    \n    // But if cExp > prodExp, the addend should be shifted LEFT, which means it has a larger\n    // effective exponent. In that case, I should use cExp as the reference instead.\n    \n    // Let me use the max exponent as reference:\n    // ref_exp = max(prodExp, cExp)  -- but I also need to account for the different mantissa sizes\n    \n    // Actually, let me think about it differently. I'll use a wide accumulator (72 bits)\n    // and position both mantissas in it based on their exponents.\n    \n    // For a number with unbiased exponent E and mantissa M (with leading 1 at the MSB):\n    // The mantissa M represents 1.xxx, where the leading 1 corresponds to 2^E.\n    // In the 72-bit accumulator, I'll position the leading 1 at a specific bit position.\n    \n    // Let's say the leading 1 of the product (at bit 46 or 47 of the 48-bit mantissa)\n    // corresponds to exponent prodExp (or prodExp + 1 if bit 47 is set).\n    \n    // And the leading 1 of the addend (at bit 23 of the 24-bit mantissa)\n    // corresponds to exponent cExp.\n    \n    // I'll position the product in the accumulator such that its leading 1 (at bit 46 or 47)\n    // is at accumulator bit 71 (the MSB). Then the addend's leading 1 should be at\n    // accumulator bit 71 - (prodExp - cExp) (if the product has the larger exponent)\n    // or at accumulator bit 71 + (cExp - prodExp) (if the addend has the larger exponent).\n    \n    // Hmm, but the product's leading 1 can be at bit 46 or 47. This is getting complicated.\n    \n    // Let me normalize the product first.\n    val prodNormalShift = s1.prodMant(47)  // 1 if product >= 2\n    val prodMantNorm = Mux(prodNormalShift, s1.prodMant, Cat(0.U(1.W), s1.prodMant(46, 0)))\n    // Now prodMantNorm has its leading 1 at bit 47\n    val prodExpNorm = Mux(prodNormalShift, s1.prodExp + 1.S, s1.prodExp)\n    // Now the product is normalized: value = (-1)^sign * prodMantNorm * 2^(prodExpNorm - 47)\n    \n    // And the addend: value = (-1)^sign * cMant * 2^(cExp - 23)\n    \n    // Now I need to align them. I'll use the max exponent as reference.\n    // The product's effective exponent (at the MSB of the mantissa) is prodExpNorm\n    // The addend's effective exponent (at the MSB of the mantissa) is cExp\n    \n    // I'll position both in a 72-bit accumulator where bit 71 corresponds to max(prodExpNorm, cExp)\n    \n    val expDiff = prodExpNorm - s1.cExp  // positive if product has larger exponent\n    \n    // Product position: leading 1 at bit 71 (if product has max exp) or shifted right\n    // Addend position: leading 1 at bit 71 (if addend has max exp) or shifted right\n    \n    // For simplicity, let me position the product's leading 1 at bit 71\n    // Then the addend's leading 1 should be at bit 71 - expDiff\n    \n    // Product in accumulator: prodMantNorm is 48 bits with leading 1 at bit 47\n    // Position at bit 71: shift left by (71 - 47) = 24\n    val prodInAcc = Cat(prodMantNorm, 0.U(24.W))  // 72 bits\n    \n    // Addend: cMant is 24 bits with leading 1 at bit 23\n    // Should be positioned at bit (71 - expDiff)\n    // Currently at bit 23, need to shift left by (71 - expDiff - 23) = 48 - expDiff\n    // But this could be negative (if addend has much larger exponent)\n    \n    // Let me use a different approach: shift the addend relative to the product.\n    // The product's leading 1 is at accumulator bit 71.\n    // The addend's leading 1 should be at accumulator bit 71 - expDiff.\n    \n    // Since cMant has its leading 1 at bit 23, I need to shift it so that the leading 1\n    // is at bit 71 - expDiff.\n    // Shift amount = (71 - expDiff) - 23 = 48 - expDiff\n    // If expDiff is small (product has larger exponent), shift amount is large (addend shifted right)\n    // If expDiff is large (addend has larger exponent), shift amount is small or negative\n    \n    // This doesn't work well for negative shift amounts. Let me use a dynamic shift.\n    \n    // Actually, the simplest approach is to compute the shift amount as a signed value and\n    // handle both left and right shifts.\n    \n    // But dynamic left shifts in Chisel are tricky. Let me just implement a working FMA.\n\nLet me use a very practical approach:\n1. Compute the product of a and b as a 48-bit mantissa with a known exponent\n2. Normalize the product to have the leading 1 at a known bit position\n3. Align the addend with the product\n4. Add using sign-magnitude arithmetic (compare, subtract smaller from larger)\n5. Normalize the result\n6. Round to nearest even\n\nFor the implementation, I'll use 72-bit wide arithmetic for the addition step.\n\nLet me code this up now. I'll keep it simple and correct.\n\nActually, let me simplify even further. Since the intermediate states (FmaState1, FmaState2) aren't directly checked by the tests, I can define them however I want. The only thing that matters is that FmaStage3 produces the correct Fp32 result.\n\nSo I could even implement the entire FMA in FmaStage3 and just pass through the inputs in stages 1 and 2. But that would defeat the purpose of having pipeline stages. Let me implement it properly.\n\nOK let me just write the code now. I'll implement it as three combinational stages.\n\nLet me define the states as:\n\nFmaState1:\n- Product sign, exponent (unbiased, SInt), mantissa (48 bits)\n- Addend sign, exponent (unbiased, SInt), mantissa (24 bits with leading 1)\n- Special case flags (NaN, Inf, Zero)\n\nFmaState2:\n- Result sign, exponent (unbiased, SInt), mantissa (72 bits, left-aligned)\n- Is zero, is special, special result\n\nFmaStage3:\n- Normalize the 72-bit mantissa to 24 bits\n- Round to nearest even\n- Pack into Fp32\n\nLet me code this up:\n\n```scala\npackage common\n\nimport chisel3._\nimport s.cMant := Cat(1.U(1.W), c.mantissa)  // 24 bits\n    s.cZero := cZero\n    \n    // NaN detection\n    val zeroTimesInf = (aZero && bInf) || (aInf && bZero)\n    val infMinusInf = ((aInf || bInf) &&
